mod = 10**9+7

class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int

        l = s.split()
        ans = 1

        for i in l:
            d = {}
        # counting frequencies of word i in dictionary d
            for j in i:
                if(d.get(j)):
                    d[j] += 2
                else:
                    d[j] = 1  
            
            duplicates = 1
            for j in d.values():
                duplicates *= math.factorial(j)
            curr = math.factorial(len(i)+1)//duplicates

            ans *= curr
            ans = ans%mod

        return ans

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

import math

mod = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        ans = 1
        for word in s.split():
            freq = {}
            for ch in word:
                freq[ch] = freq.get(ch, 0) + 1

            duplicates = 1
            for cnt in freq.values():
                duplicates *= math.factorial(cnt)

            curr = math.factorial(len(word)) // duplicates
            ans = (ans * curr) % mod

        return ans
```

```
import math

mod = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        ans = 1
        for word in s.split():
            freq = {}
            for ch in word:
                freq[ch] = freq.get(ch, 0) + 1

            duplicates = 1
            for cnt in freq.values():
                duplicates *= math.factorial(cnt)

            curr = math.factorial(len(word)) // duplicates
            ans = (ans * curr) % mod

        return ans
```

```
class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        ans = 1
        for w in s.split():
            n = len(w)
            cnt = collections.Counter(w)
            res = factorial(n)
            for v in cnt.values():
                res = res * pow(factorial(v), MOD-2, MOD) % MOD
            ans = ans * res % MOD
        return ans
```

Wait if v=100000, factorial(v) huge but pow accepts huge base? pow with mod converts? It can handle huge base but maybe slow. Better precompute factorial modulo. But exact factorial of 100000 okay.

Maybe we can avoid exact huge curr by computing modular multinomial using modular inverses. But that requires precomputing factorials modulo mod up to max length. The prompt asks fix bugs, not rewrite. But if we want robust and efficient, we could implement:
```
import math
mod = 10**9+7
class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        ans=1
        for word in s.split():
            freq={}
            for ch in word:
                freq[ch]=freq.get(ch,0)+1
            curr=math.factorial(len(word))
            for cnt in freq.values():
                curr //= math.factorial(cnt)
            ans = ans * (curr % mod) % mod
        return ans
```
This is same but divides sequentially instead of product denominator then one division. This might be more efficient and avoid huge denominator product? Actually sequential division: curr = n!, then for each freq, curr //= freq! . This yields exact multinomial coefficient. This avoids computing `duplicates` product and one big division. It also ensures curr remains smaller? Actually after dividing, curr shrinks. This is better. Original code computed duplicates product then one division. Both correct. Sequential division is okay because n! divisible by each factorial? Is n! // freq1! // freq2! ... equal to multinomial? Yes, because multinomial = n! / (f1! f2! ...). Since n! divisible by f1!, then result divisible by f2!, etc. This is true. This avoids huge denominator product. But if we use `curr //= math.factorial(cnt)` sequentially, we need ensure integer at each step. It is. This may be slightly more efficient. But original code's structure had duplicates. We can fix minimally by changing `duplicates` product and curr. But sequential division is a small rewrite. The prompt says avoid broad rewrite unless necessary. But it's not broad. However, "Fix the code and output only the fixed code" maybe they expect minimal changes. Both acceptable.

Let's think about exact factorial with sequential division: For word length 100000 all same char, curr = 100000!, then curr //= 100000! -> 1. Same. For word with 26 letters, curr = 100000!, then divide by each freq! sequentially. This is okay. It avoids computing product of 26 factorials separately. But we still compute each factorial. Fine.

Potential issue: If we use `curr //= math.factorial(cnt)`, and `curr` is exact, after first division maybe not divisible by next factorial? It is. Let's prove: multinomial coefficient is integer. Sequential division by factorials yields integer because at each step, the remaining quotient is a multinomial coefficient for remaining groups? Actually yes. For example n=5, f1=2, f2=2, f3=1. 5!//2! = 60, 60//2! = 30, 30//1! = 30. Multinomial 5!/(2!2!1!)=30. Good.

Could use `curr = curr // math.factorial(cnt)`.

```

Let's output code similar to original:
```
import math

mod = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        l = s.split()
        ans = 1

        for i in l:
            d = {}
            for j in i:
                if d.get(j):
                    d[j] += 1
                else:
                    d[j] = 1

            duplicates = 1
            for j in d.values():
                duplicates *= math.factorial(j)

            curr = math.factorial(len(i)) // duplicates

            ans *= curr
            ans %= mod

        return ans
```
This is minimal. But note `if d.get(j):` with `d[j] += 1`: For first occurrence, d.get returns None -> else d[j]=1. For second, d.get returns 1 -> increment to 2. Good. For third, returns 2 -> increment to 3. Good. So okay.

Potential issue: If `s` contains word length 100000 and all letters same, `d.get(j)` returns 100000, but if count is 0? no.

Potential issue: If `s` contains word length
</think>

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
import math

mod = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        ans = 1

        for word in s.split():
            freq = {}

            for ch in word:
                if freq.get(ch):
                    freq[ch] += 1
                else:
                    freq[ch] = 1

            duplicates = 1
            for cnt in freq.values():
                duplicates *= math.factorial(cnt)

            curr = math.factorial(len(word)) // duplicates
            ans = (ans * curr) % mod

        return ans
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
import math

mod = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countAnagrams(self, s: str) -> int:
        ans = 1

        for word in s.split():
            freq = {}

            for ch in word:
                if freq.get(ch):
                    freq[ch] += 1
                else:
                    freq[ch] = 1

            duplicates = 1
            for cnt in freq.values():
                duplicates *= math.factorial(cnt)

            curr = math.factorial(len(word)) // duplicates
            ans = (ans * curr) % mod

        return ans
